{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01NtcbFjrvfXXhYdezkJ8PGH","type":"tool_result","content":"1249\t\n1250\t\\begin{table}[h]\n1251\t\\centering\n1252\t\\small\n1253\t\\caption{PPI++ RMSE reduction (\\%) vs.\\ gold label fraction across five topologies. Negative values indicate PPI++ increases RMSE relative to the naive estimator.}\n1254\t\\label{tab:ppi_gold_ratio}\n1255\t\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n1256\t\\toprule\n1257\tTopology & 5\\% & 10\\% & 20\\% & 50\\% & 100\\% \\\\\n1258\t\\midrule\n1259\tConfounding & $+$84.8 & $+$88.6 & $+$91.8 & $+$94.2 & $+$95.2 \\\\\n1260\tExposure & $+$75.2 & $+$83.1 & $+$87.1 & $+$91.2 & $+$93.8 \\\\\n1261\tMediation & $+$56.7 & $+$70.4 & $+$77.2 & $+$85.3 & $+$88.7 \\\\\n1262\tIV & $+$1.4 & $+$22.1 & $+$50.1 & $+$66.7 & $+$76.4 \\\\\n1263\tM-bias & $-$125.3 & $-$43.6 & $-$19.6 & $+$6.0 & $+$18.5 \\\\\n1264\t\\bottomrule\n1265\t\\end{tabular}\n1266\t\\end{table}\n1267\t\n1268\tFor confounding and exposure, PPI++ achieves $>$75\\% RMSE reduction even at 5\\% gold labels; IV requires ${\\geq}20\\%$ gold to reach 50\\% reduction, reflecting the Wald estimator's higher variance. M-bias exhibits harmful correction at ${\\leq}20\\%$ gold ($-125\\%$ at 5\\%), because PPI++ re-exposes the M-bias pathway that annotation error had attenuated; only at ${\\geq}50\\%$ gold does the estimator become weakly beneficial ($+6\\%$).\n1269\t\n1270\t\\subsection{Realistic Differential Misclassification Thresholds}\n1271\t\\label{sec:realistic_epsilon}\n1272\t\n1273\tOur non-differential assumption (Definition~\\ref{def:nondiff}) requires $P(A^*{\\mid}A,Y) = P(A^*{\\mid}A)$. We estimate how much $Y$-stratified differential error each of our 23 empirical LLM confusion matrices can tolerate before exposure sign reversal exceeds 5\\%. For each matrix $C$, we construct $C_{\\text{high}} = \\text{renorm}(C + \\varepsilon\\Delta)$ and $C_{\\text{low}} = \\text{renorm}(C - \\varepsilon\\Delta)$ with random off-diagonal perturbation $\\Delta$ (500 MC trials per $\\varepsilon$), sweep $\\varepsilon \\in [0, 0.50]$, and report the safe threshold $\\varepsilon^*_{\\text{safe}} = \\max\\{\\varepsilon : \\text{reversal rate} < 5\\%\\}$ (Table~\\ref{tab:realistic_epsilon}).\n1274\t\n1275\t\\begin{table}[h]\n1276\t\\centering\n1277\t\\small\n1278\t\\caption{Safe differential misclassification thresholds $\\varepsilon^*_{\\text{safe}}$ by dataset/K. For $K{=}2$ (binary), no configuration produces sign reversal up to $\\varepsilon{=}0.50$. For $K{\\geq}3$, the majority of configurations are fragile ($\\varepsilon^*_{\\text{safe}}{=}0$), confirming that the non-differential assumption is load-bearing for multi-class exposure.}\n1279\t\\label{tab:realistic_epsilon}\n1280\t\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n1281\t\\toprule\n1282\t\\textbf{Dataset} & $K$ & $n$ & \\textbf{Mean} $\\varepsilon^*$ & \\textbf{Median} $\\varepsilon^*$ & $\\varepsilon^*{=}0$ \\\\\n1283\t\\midrule\n1284\tCivil Comments & 2 & 7 & 0.50 & 0.50 & 0/7 \\\\\n1285\tVAST & 3 & 8 & 0.003 & 0.00 & 7/8 \\\\\n1286\tHuMAID & 10 & 8 & 0.045 & 0.00 & 7/8 \\\\\n1287\t\\bottomrule\n1288\t\\end{tabular}\n1289\t\\end{table}\n1290\t\n1291\tThe distribution is sharply bimodal: all $K{=}2$ configurations are immune to differential misclassification within the tested range ($\\varepsilon^*_{\\text{safe}}{=}0.50$), consistent with the binary sign-preservation guarantee (Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip}(c)). For $K{\\geq}3$, 14 of 16 configurations have $\\varepsilon^*_{\\text{safe}}{=}0$---sign reversal occurs at the smallest tested $\\varepsilon{=}0.01$. Two outliers (VAST: $\\varepsilon^*{=}0.02$; HuMAID: $\\varepsilon^*{=}0.36$) have atypically structured confusion matrices. This confirms that even small violations of non-differential misclassification are consequential for multi-class exposure, underscoring the practical importance of validating $P(A^*{\\mid}A,Y) \\approx P(A^*{\\mid}A)$ on gold-labeled subsets when $K \\geq 3$.\n1292\t\n1293\t\\subsection{Proportional Effects with Empirical Confusion Matrices}\n1294\t\\label{sec:proportional_real_matrices}\n1295\t\n1296\tTheorem~\\ref{thm:monotone} assumes proportional effects ($f = \\lambda g$). We evaluate this assumption using all 23 empirical LLM confusion matrices under four DGP groups in the mediation topology (23 matrices $\\times$ 50 seeds $\\times$ 100{,}000 precomputed samples per seed).\n1297\t\n1298\t\\begin{table}[h]\n1299\t\\centering\n1300\t\\small\n1301\t\\caption{Proportional effects validation with 23 real LLM confusion matrices under mediation DGP. $\\lambda(C) \\in [0,1]$: proportion satisfying the Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotone} bound. Sign pres.: sign preservation rate of the direct effect estimate.}\n1302\t\\label{tab:proportional_empirical}\n1303\t\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n1304\t\\toprule\n1305\t\\textbf{DGP Group} & $\\lambda(C)$ \\textbf{mean} & $\\lambda(C) \\in [0,1]$ & \\textbf{Sign pres.} & \\textbf{CV} \\\\\n1306\t\\midrule\n1307\tProportional ($f{=}\\lambda g$) & 0.271 & 99.8\\% & 100\\% & 0.00 \\\\\n1308\tNear-prop.\\ ($\\sigma{=}0.1$) & 0.285 & 99.2\\% & 100\\% & 1.06 \\\\\n1309\tNon-proportional & 0.240 & 85.0\\% & 97.3\\% & 3.60 \\\\\n1310\tSparse & 0.296 & 75.8\\% & 98.6\\% & 6.59 \\\\\n1311\t\\bottomrule\n1312\t\\end{tabular}\n1313\t\\end{table}\n1314\t\n1315\tUnder proportional and near-proportional effects, $\\lambda(C)$ remains within $[0,1]$ for ${\\geq}99\\%$ of configurations with 100\\% sign preservation---the Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotone} conditions are satisfied by real LLM confusion matrices. Even under non-proportional effects ($f$ independent of $g$), sign preservation remains at 97.3\\%, with $\\lambda(C)$ exceeding $[0,1]$ in 15\\% of cases. The per-$K$ breakdown shows that $\\lambda(C)$ is dimension-dependent: $K{=}2$ (mean 0.249), $K{=}3$ (mean 0.082), $K{=}10$ (mean 0.490), with 100\\% in $[0,1]$ across all $K$ under proportional effects. Sparse DGP (zero-effect categories) produces the highest violation rate (24.2\\%), consistent with the breakdown mechanism: zero-effect categories make $f/g$ ratios undefined, maximally departing from proportionality.\n1316\t\n1317\t\\subsection{Case Study: ASV Diagnosis of Political Ad Tone Classification}\n1318\t\\label{sec:asv_case_study}\n1319\t\n1320\tWe illustrate the ASV diagnostic workflow on a concrete CSS application. \\citet{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023} use LLM classifiers to annotate political advertising tone ($K{=}3$: promote/contrast/attack) from the Fowler \\& Ridout database, then estimate the causal effect of ad tone on voter outcomes. The annotated variable is the \\textbf{exposure} (treatment). Using a representative $K{=}3$ confusion matrix from our empirical set (VAST DeepSeek zero-shot, mean diagonal 0.734) as a proxy:\n1321\t\n1322\t\\begin{center}\n1323\t\\small\n1324\t\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n1325\t\\toprule\n1326\tCoefficient & $\\delta^*_{\\text{mag}}$ (10\\%) & $\\delta^*_{\\text{sf}}$ & Relative bias at $\\delta{=}1$ \\\\\n1327\t\\midrule\n1328\t$\\beta_1$ (primary) & 0.234 & $> 1.5$ & 39.7\\% \\\\\n1329\t$\\beta_2$ (secondary) & 0.154 & $> 1.5$ & 60.4\\% \\\\\n1330\t\\bottomrule\n1331\t\\end{tabular}\n1332\t\\end{center}\n1333\t\n1334\tBoth coefficients are magnitude-unsafe ($\\delta^* < 1$), with the secondary coefficient more fragile ($\\delta^*{=}0.154$). If the same annotator were instead classifying a \\textbf{confounder} (e.g., media framing as a control variable), magnitude ASV rises to $\\delta^*{=}0.690$---a $3{\\times}$ increase in error tolerance from the same confusion matrix. This demonstrates the central insight: topology, not annotator quality, determines vulnerability. Algorithm~\\ref{alg:correction} routes this exposure topology to PPI++ or DSL correction; matrix-based methods (naive plug-in, MLA) are ineffective due to FWL non-applicability.\n1335\t\n1336\t\\subsection{End-to-End Demonstration}\n1337\t\\label{sec:e2e_demo}\n1338\t\n1339\t\\input{e2e_demo_table.tex}\n1340\t\n1341\tThe demonstration uses a symmetric $C_0$ ($d_{\\mathrm{rel}}{=}0$); empirical $K{=}3$ matrices deviate substantially (median $d_{\\mathrm{rel}}{=}0.33$, Remark~\\ref{rem:symmetry}), but core ASV ranking and correction routing remain valid under Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}.\n1342\t\n1343\t\\subsection{Semi-Real Case Study with Production LLM Confusion Matrices}\n1344\t\\label{sec:real_cm_demo}\n1345\t\n1346\tWe repeat the end-to-end pipeline using 10 confusion matrices estimated from production LLM annotation of VAST stance detection ($K{=}3$; DeepSeek, Llama4, Qwen2.5-7B, Qwen3 under zero-shot and few-shot prompting). These matrices exhibit median $d_{\\mathrm{rel}}{=}0.33$ (Remark~\\ref{rem:symmetry}), far from the symmetric assumption of Theorem~\\ref{thm:k3_symmetric}. Results confirm both topology-dependent patterns: PPI++ achieves 93.6\\% median exposure bias reduction while plug-in corrections yield 0\\% improvement for confounding (FWL invariance, machine precision; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e-real-cm}).\n1347\t\n1348\t\\input{e2e_real_cm_table.tex}\n1349\t\n1350\t\\subsection{Correction Method Sensitivity to $K$}\n1351\t\\label{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}\n1352\t\n1353\tWe test whether the correction method ranking from Table~\\ref{tab:correction} ($K{=}3$) is stable at $K{=}5$ and $K{=}10$. For each $K$, we sample 5 random Dirichlet confusion matrices (diagonal $\\geq 0.6$), run 100 MC replications per topology--matrix combination ($N{=}5{,}000$, 10\\% gold fraction), and compute RMSE reduction for all five methods across all seven topologies.\n1354\t\n1355\t\\paragraph{Grand ranking stability.}\n1356\tSpearman rank correlations across all topology--method pairs: $\\rho_{3,5} = 0.80$, $\\rho_{3,10} = 0.84$, $\\rho_{5,10} = 0.93$ (all $p < 10^{-8}$). Per-topology correlations are even stronger: 5 of 7 topologies (confounding, collider, M-bias, IV, and exposure at $K{=}10$) achieve $\\rho = 1.0$ between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}10$.\n1357\t\n1358\t\\paragraph{Method-specific patterns.}\n1359\tPPI++ effectiveness degrades with $K$: confounding RMSE reduction drops from 85.6\\% ($K{=}3$) to 75.6\\% ($K{=}10$); mediation drops from 57.1\\% to 9.0\\%. At $K{=}10$, PPI++ becomes harmful for IV ($-124\\%$) and exposure ($-11\\%$). MC-SIMEX is the most $K$-robust method, maintaining the top rank for 5/7 topologies at $K{=}10$ (vs.\\ 3/7 at $K{=}3$). FWL invariance (naive plug-in and MLA achieving 0\\% bias reduction on the treatment coefficient) holds at all $K$ tested, confirming the structural nature of this result.\n1360\t\n1361\t\\begin{table}[h]\n1362\t\\centering\n1363\t\\small\n1364\t\\caption{Top correction method and RMSE reduction (\\%) by topology across $K$. Bold = top method for that topology--$K$ combination.}\n1365\t\\label{tab:correction_k}\n1366\t\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n1367\t\\toprule\n1368\t\\textbf{Topology} & $K{=}3$ & $K{=}5$ & $K{=}10$ \\\\\n1369\t\\midrule\n1370\tConfounding & \\textbf{PPI++} 85.6 & \\textbf{PPI++} 76.4 & \\textbf{PPI++} 75.6 \\\\\n1371\tMediation & \\textbf{PPI++} 57.1 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 59.7 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 59.0 \\\\\n1372\tCollider & \\textbf{SIMEX} 55.4 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 63.4 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 57.3 \\\\\n1373\tExposure & \\textbf{SIMEX} 72.5 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 65.0 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 47.3 \\\\\n1374\tM-bias & \\textbf{SIMEX} 3.2 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 47.0 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 43.7 \\\\\n1375\tIV & \\textbf{SIMEX} 30.9 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 30.4 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 27.1 \\\\\n1376\tFront-door & \\textbf{PPI++} 82.9 & \\textbf{PPI++} 69.0 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 70.1 \\\\\n1377\t\\bottomrule\n1378\t\\end{tabular}\n1379\t\\end{table}\n1380\t\n1381\t\\subsection{Comparison with Related Sensitivity Frameworks}\n1382\t\\label{sec:sensitivity_comparison}\n1383\t\n1384\t\\begin{table}[h]\n1385\t\\centering\n1386\t\\caption{Comparison of sensitivity frameworks.}\n1387\t\\label{tab:sensitivity_comparison}\n1388\t\\small\n1389\t\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n1390\t\\toprule\n1391\t& \\textbf{ASV} & \\textbf{E-value} & \\textbf{Partial} $R^2$ \\\\\n1392\t\\midrule\n1393\tParameter space & $K{\\times}K$ confusion $C(\\delta)$ & Scalar confounder & $R^2$ of omitted var \\\\\n1394\tSensitivity path & $(1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$ & Monotone scalar & Monotone scalar \\\\\n1395\tTopology-specific & \\checkmark & \\ding{55} & \\ding{55} \\\\\n1396\tSign-flip diagnostic & \\checkmark ($\\delta^*_{\\text{sign}}$) & \\ding{55} & \\ding{55} \\\\\n1397\tTopology dependence & \\checkmark (7 topologies ranked) & \\ding{55} & \\ding{55} \\\\\n1398\tMonotonicity & Proved $K{=}2$; conj.\\ $K{\\geq}3$ & Guaranteed & Guaranteed \\\\\n1399\tTarget & Annotation error & Unmeasured confounding & Omitted variable \\\\\n1400\t\\bottomrule\n1401\t\\end{tabular}\n1402\t\\end{table}\n1403\t\n1404\t\\subsection{Per-Topology $F_1$--Bias Correlation}\n1405\t\\label{sec:within_topo_f1}\n1406\t\n1407\tThe cross-topology correlation between macro $F_1$ and $|\\text{bias}|$ is weak ($\\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$; \\S\\ref{sec:empirical}), but decomposing by topology reveals a structured pattern. Table~\\ref{tab:within_topo_f1} reports Spearman correlations computed over 23 LLM configurations (HumAID $K{=}10$, VAST $K{=}3$, CivilComments $K{=}2$; 161 total scenarios).\n1408\t\n1409\t\\begin{table}[h]\n1410\t\\centering\n1411\t\\small\n1412\t\\caption{Within-topology Spearman $\\rho(F_1, |\\text{bias}|)$ across 23 empirical LLM configurations $\\times$ 3 datasets. Negative $\\rho$: higher $F_1$ $\\to$ lower bias (expected). Positive $\\rho$: higher $F_1$ $\\to$ higher bias (protective attenuation removed).}\n1413\t\\label{tab:within_topo_f1}\n1414\t\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n1415\t\\toprule\n1416\t\\textbf{Topology} & $n$ & $\\rho$ & $p$ \\\\\n1417\t\\midrule\n1418\tConfounding & 23 & $-0.557$ & 0.006 \\\\\n1419\tMediation & 23 & $-0.565$ & 0.005 \\\\\n1420\tExposure & 23 & $-0.531$ & 0.009 \\\\\n1421\tFront-door & 23 & $-0.568$ & 0.005 \\\\\n1422\tIV & 23 & $-0.700$ & $<$0.001 \\\\\n1423\tCollider & 23 & $+0.310$ & 0.150 \\\\\n1424\tM-bias & 23 & $+0.867$ & $<$0.001 \\\\\n1425\t\\bottomrule\n1426\t\\end{tabular}\n1427\t\\end{table}\n1428\t\n1429\tFive topologies show the expected negative correlation: better annotation quality ($F_1{\\uparrow}$) reduces causal bias. IV shows the strongest effect ($\\rho = -0.70$), consistent with the Wald estimator's sensitivity to misclassification in the first stage. M-bias uniquely reverses: $\\rho = +0.87$ ($p < 0.001$). The mechanism is that misclassification attenuates the spurious M-bias path, so the naive estimator under noisy annotations is \\emph{closer} to the true causal effect; improving annotation quality (higher $F_1$) removes this protective attenuation and \\emph{increases} bias. This is consistent with the correction results (\\S\\ref{sec:correction}), where PPI++ harms M-bias ($-$88\\%) by removing the same protective noise. Collider shows a positive but non-significant trend ($\\rho = +0.31$, $p = 0.15$), consistent with Berkson bias attenuation. Per-dataset breakdowns (CivilComments $K{=}2$: $\\rho = -1.0$ for all topologies with $n{=}7$) should be interpreted with caution given the small sample and limited $F_1$ variation within the binary task.\n1430\t\n1431\t\\subsection{Confusion Matrix Estimation Robustness}\n1432\t\\label{sec:c_robustness}\n1433\t\n1434\tThe ASV framework requires the confusion matrix $C$, which in practice must be estimated from a held-out gold-labeled subset. We assess how estimation noise in $\\hat{C}$ propagates to the safe/fragile partition and ASV ranking. For each of 8 empirical confusion matrices (VAST, 4 LLMs $\\times$ 2 prompting strategies), we draw $n_{\\text{gold}} \\in \\{50, 100, 200, 500\\}$ samples from a $\\text{Multinomial}(n_{\\text{gold}}, C_{\\cdot k})$ distribution per column, compute $\\hat{C}$, and evaluate the resulting ASV ranking across 200 bootstrap replicates.\n1435\t\n1436\t\\begin{table}[h]\n1437\t\\centering\n1438\t\\small\n1439\t\\caption{$\\hat{C}$ estimation robustness: safe/fragile partition stability and ASV ranking correlation (Spearman $\\rho$ vs.\\ true-$C$ ranking) under multinomial bootstrap from finite gold sets. 8 empirical matrices $\\times$ 200 bootstrap replicates per $n_{\\text{gold}}$.}\n1440\t\\label{tab:c_robustness}\n1441\t\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n1442\t\\toprule\n1443\t$n_{\\text{gold}}$ & Partition stability & $\\rho$ (median) & $\\rho$ ($q_{25}$) \\\\\n1444\t\\midrule\n1445\t50 & 50.4\\% & 0.991 & 0.789 \\\\\n1446\t100 & 82.0\\% & 1.000 & 0.991 \\\\\n1447\t200 & 85.6\\% & 1.000 & 0.991 \\\\\n1448\t500 & 88.5\\% & 1.000 & 0.991 \\\\\n1449\t\\bottomrule\n1450\t\\end{tabular}\n1451\t\\end{table}\n1452\t\n1453\tThe ASV ranking is highly robust: median Spearman $\\rho \\geq 0.99$ even at $n_{\\text{gold}}{=}50$. The safe/fragile partition (5 safe vs.\\ 2 fragile topologies) recovers reliably from $n_{\\text{gold}} \\geq 100$ (82\\% stability), increasing to 89\\% at $n_{\\text{gold}}{=}500$. The remaining instability concentrates in source matrices with low diagonal dominance (e.g., VAST DeepSeek few-shot: $\\min C_{kk} = 0.16$), where bootstrap noise can shift borderline ASV values across the partition boundary. All 8 truth partitions match the canonical safe/fragile partition, confirming that the theoretical dichotomy holds empirically.\n1454\t\n1455\t\\subsection{Sign Reversal Determinants}\n1456\t\\label{sec:sign_determinants}\n1457\t\n1458\tWe investigate which confusion matrix features predict exposure sign reversal across the 23 empirical LLM configurations. For each matrix, we compute 10 structural features: $\\min_k C_{kk}$ (minimum diagonal), $\\overline{C_{kk}}$ (mean diagonal), $\\max_{j \\neq k} C_{jk}$ (maximum off-diagonal), $\\max_{j \\neq k} C_{jk}/C_{kk}$ (off-diagonal ratio), $\\log(\\kappa(C))$ (log condition number), mean and max column entropy, spectral gap, $\\det(C)$, and minimum singular value. Sign reversal is defined as any exposure coefficient reversing sign under misclassification.\n1459\t\n1460\t\\begin{table}[h]\n1461\t\\centering\n1462\t\\small\n1463\t\\caption{Sign reversal rates by $K$ and best single-feature decision rule. Rule: $\\det(C) < 0.014 \\to$ sign reversal (accuracy 91.3\\%, sensitivity 85.7\\%, specificity 100\\%). An AND rule ($\\min_k C_{kk} < 0.55$ and mean entropy $> 0.40$) achieves 95.7\\% accuracy with 100\\% sensitivity.}\n1464\t\\label{tab:sign_determinants}\n1465\t\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n1466\t\\toprule\n1467\t$K$ & $n$ & Sign reversal rate \\\\\n1468\t\\midrule\n1469\t2 & 7 & 0/7 (0\\%) \\\\\n1470\t3 & 8 & 7/8 (87.5\\%) \\\\\n1471\t10 & 8 & 7/8 (87.5\\%) \\\\\n1472\t\\bottomrule\n1473\t\\end{tabular}\n1474\t\\end{table}\n1475\t\n1476\tBinary matrices ($K{=}2$) never produce exposure sign reversal, consistent with classical attenuation theory. For $K \\geq 3$, the determinant of $C$ is the strongest single predictor: $\\det(C) < 0.014$ identifies sign-reversing matrices with 100\\% specificity (zero false positives) and 85.7\\% sensitivity. An AND rule combining $\\min_k C_{kk} < 0.55$ with mean column entropy $> 0.40$ achieves 100\\% sensitivity at 88.9\\% specificity. These rules are robust to $\\beta$-scaling (accuracy unchanged at $0.3{\\times}$ and $3{\\times}$ effect magnitude) but sensitive to $\\beta$-sign structure: under uniform-positive $\\beta$ (all coefficients same sign), no matrices produce sign reversal regardless of $C$, as cancellation across differently-signed coefficients is the underlying mechanism.\n1477\t\n1478\t\\subsection{Y-Stratified Non-Differential Verification}\n1479\t\\label{sec:y_stratified_test}\n1480\t\n1481\tWe directly test the non-differential assumption (Definition~\\ref{def:nondiff}) by stratifying confusion matrices by outcome-proxy variables and testing $C(Y{=}\\text{high}) \\approx C(Y{=}\\text{low})$. Using 12 LLM configurations on VAST ($K{=}3$; 4 models $\\times$ 3 prompting strategies, 1{,}500 samples each), we compute per-stratum confusion matrices under three Y-proxies and apply permutation tests ($B{=}10{,}000$, test statistic: $\\max_{j,k} |C^{(0)}_{jk} - C^{(1)}_{jk}|$).\n1482\t\n1483\t\\begin{table}[h]\n1484\t\\centering\n1485\t\\small\n1486\t\\caption{Y-stratified non-differential test. Content-independent Y (text length) shows no significant violation (1/12 rejection $\\approx$ expected false positive rate at $\\alpha{=}0.05$). Content-dependent proxies (topic, sentiment) show expected violations since LLMs observe text content.}\n1487\t\\label{tab:y_stratified}\n1488\t\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n1489\t\\toprule\n1490\t\\textbf{Y proxy} & \\textbf{Type} & \\textbf{Reject ($\\alpha{=}0.05$)} & \\textbf{Median $p$} \\\\\n1491\t\\midrule\n1492\tText length & Content-indep. & 1/12 & 0.490 \\\\\n1493\tTopic & Content-dep. & 11/12 & 0.002 \\\\\n1494\tSentiment & Content-dep. & 10/12 & 0.017 \\\\\n1495\t\\bottomrule\n1496\t\\end{tabular}\n1497\t\\end{table}\n1498\t\n1499\tThe single text-length rejection (Llama-4 few-shot-3, $p{=}0.027$) is consistent with the expected false positive rate ($0.05 \\times 12 = 0.6$). Content-dependent proxies (topic, sentiment) show systematic rejection because LLMs process text content from which these variables can be inferred, violating $Y \\perp \\text{text} \\mid A$. In causal inference applications, $Y$ is typically an external outcome (e.g., health status, economic indicator) that cannot be inferred from the annotated text, satisfying the independence condition. The content-dependent rejections confirm statistical power: the test can detect violations when they exist.\n1500\t\n1501\t\\section{Notation}\n1502\t\\label{app:notation_table}\n1503\t\n1504\t\\begin{table}[h]\n1505\t\\centering\n1506\t\\small\n1507\t\\caption{Key notation used throughout the paper.}\n1508\t\\label{tab:notation}\n1509\t\\begin{tabular}{cl}\n1510\t\\toprule\n1511\t\\textbf{Symbol} & \\textbf{Description} \\\\\n1512\t\\midrule\n1513\t$K$ & Number of annotation categories \\\\\n1514\t$C$ & Column-stochastic confusion matrix, $C_{jk} = P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k)$ \\\\\n1515\t$A$, $A^*$ & True and observed (misclassified) annotation \\\\\n1516\t$T$ & Treatment variable \\\\\n1517\t$Y$ & Outcome variable \\\\\n1518\t$\\tau$ & Causal effect of interest \\\\"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"f8874594-f47e-4acf-8bb7-880f8df82032","uuid":"5a8d71ce-ff9e-4917-b051-dcacacbcb35b","timestamp":"2026-05-24T07:30:54.273Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/appendix_proofs.tex","content":"\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{PPI++ RMSE reduction (\\%) vs.\\ gold label fraction across five topologies. Negative values indicate PPI++ increases RMSE relative to the naive estimator.}\n\\label{tab:ppi_gold_ratio}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nTopology & 5\\% & 10\\% & 20\\% & 50\\% & 100\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & $+$84.8 & $+$88.6 & $+$91.8 & $+$94.2 & $+$95.2 \\\\\nExposure & $+$75.2 & $+$83.1 & $+$87.1 & $+$91.2 & $+$93.8 \\\\\nMediation & $+$56.7 & $+$70.4 & $+$77.2 & $+$85.3 & $+$88.7 \\\\\nIV & $+$1.4 & $+$22.1 & $+$50.1 & $+$66.7 & $+$76.4 \\\\\nM-bias & $-$125.3 & $-$43.6 & $-$19.6 & $+$6.0 & $+$18.5 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nFor confounding and exposure, PPI++ achieves $>$75\\% RMSE reduction even at 5\\% gold labels; IV requires ${\\geq}20\\%$ gold to reach 50\\% reduction, reflecting the Wald estimator's higher variance. M-bias exhibits harmful correction at ${\\leq}20\\%$ gold ($-125\\%$ at 5\\%), because PPI++ re-exposes the M-bias pathway that annotation error had attenuated; only at ${\\geq}50\\%$ gold does the estimator become weakly beneficial ($+6\\%$).\n\n\\subsection{Realistic Differential Misclassification Thresholds}\n\\label{sec:realistic_epsilon}\n\nOur non-differential assumption (Definition~\\ref{def:nondiff}) requires $P(A^*{\\mid}A,Y) = P(A^*{\\mid}A)$. We estimate how much $Y$-stratified differential error each of our 23 empirical LLM confusion matrices can tolerate before exposure sign reversal exceeds 5\\%. For each matrix $C$, we construct $C_{\\text{high}} = \\text{renorm}(C + \\varepsilon\\Delta)$ and $C_{\\text{low}} = \\text{renorm}(C - \\varepsilon\\Delta)$ with random off-diagonal perturbation $\\Delta$ (500 MC trials per $\\varepsilon$), sweep $\\varepsilon \\in [0, 0.50]$, and report the safe threshold $\\varepsilon^*_{\\text{safe}} = \\max\\{\\varepsilon : \\text{reversal rate} < 5\\%\\}$ (Table~\\ref{tab:realistic_epsilon}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Safe differential misclassification thresholds $\\varepsilon^*_{\\text{safe}}$ by dataset/K. For $K{=}2$ (binary), no configuration produces sign reversal up to $\\varepsilon{=}0.50$. For $K{\\geq}3$, the majority of configurations are fragile ($\\varepsilon^*_{\\text{safe}}{=}0$), confirming that the non-differential assumption is load-bearing for multi-class exposure.}\n\\label{tab:realistic_epsilon}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & $K$ & $n$ & \\textbf{Mean} $\\varepsilon^*$ & \\textbf{Median} $\\varepsilon^*$ & $\\varepsilon^*{=}0$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCivil Comments & 2 & 7 & 0.50 & 0.50 & 0/7 \\\\\nVAST & 3 & 8 & 0.003 & 0.00 & 7/8 \\\\\nHuMAID & 10 & 8 & 0.045 & 0.00 & 7/8 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe distribution is sharply bimodal: all $K{=}2$ configurations are immune to differential misclassification within the tested range ($\\varepsilon^*_{\\text{safe}}{=}0.50$), consistent with the binary sign-preservation guarantee (Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip}(c)). For $K{\\geq}3$, 14 of 16 configurations have $\\varepsilon^*_{\\text{safe}}{=}0$---sign reversal occurs at the smallest tested $\\varepsilon{=}0.01$. Two outliers (VAST: $\\varepsilon^*{=}0.02$; HuMAID: $\\varepsilon^*{=}0.36$) have atypically structured confusion matrices. This confirms that even small violations of non-differential misclassification are consequential for multi-class exposure, underscoring the practical importance of validating $P(A^*{\\mid}A,Y) \\approx P(A^*{\\mid}A)$ on gold-labeled subsets when $K \\geq 3$.\n\n\\subsection{Proportional Effects with Empirical Confusion Matrices}\n\\label{sec:proportional_real_matrices}\n\nTheorem~\\ref{thm:monotone} assumes proportional effects ($f = \\lambda g$). We evaluate this assumption using all 23 empirical LLM confusion matrices under four DGP groups in the mediation topology (23 matrices $\\times$ 50 seeds $\\times$ 100{,}000 precomputed samples per seed).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Proportional effects validation with 23 real LLM confusion matrices under mediation DGP. $\\lambda(C) \\in [0,1]$: proportion satisfying the Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotone} bound. Sign pres.: sign preservation rate of the direct effect estimate.}\n\\label{tab:proportional_empirical}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{DGP Group} & $\\lambda(C)$ \\textbf{mean} & $\\lambda(C) \\in [0,1]$ & \\textbf{Sign pres.} & \\textbf{CV} \\\\\n\\midrule\nProportional ($f{=}\\lambda g$) & 0.271 & 99.8\\% & 100\\% & 0.00 \\\\\nNear-prop.\\ ($\\sigma{=}0.1$) & 0.285 & 99.2\\% & 100\\% & 1.06 \\\\\nNon-proportional & 0.240 & 85.0\\% & 97.3\\% & 3.60 \\\\\nSparse & 0.296 & 75.8\\% & 98.6\\% & 6.59 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nUnder proportional and near-proportional effects, $\\lambda(C)$ remains within $[0,1]$ for ${\\geq}99\\%$ of configurations with 100\\% sign preservation---the Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotone} conditions are satisfied by real LLM confusion matrices. Even under non-proportional effects ($f$ independent of $g$), sign preservation remains at 97.3\\%, with $\\lambda(C)$ exceeding $[0,1]$ in 15\\% of cases. The per-$K$ breakdown shows that $\\lambda(C)$ is dimension-dependent: $K{=}2$ (mean 0.249), $K{=}3$ (mean 0.082), $K{=}10$ (mean 0.490), with 100\\% in $[0,1]$ across all $K$ under proportional effects. Sparse DGP (zero-effect categories) produces the highest violation rate (24.2\\%), consistent with the breakdown mechanism: zero-effect categories make $f/g$ ratios undefined, maximally departing from proportionality.\n\n\\subsection{Case Study: ASV Diagnosis of Political Ad Tone Classification}\n\\label{sec:asv_case_study}\n\nWe illustrate the ASV diagnostic workflow on a concrete CSS application. \\citet{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023} use LLM classifiers to annotate political advertising tone ($K{=}3$: promote/contrast/attack) from the Fowler \\& Ridout database, then estimate the causal effect of ad tone on voter outcomes. The annotated variable is the \\textbf{exposure} (treatment). Using a representative $K{=}3$ confusion matrix from our empirical set (VAST DeepSeek zero-shot, mean diagonal 0.734) as a proxy:\n\n\\begin{center}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nCoefficient & $\\delta^*_{\\text{mag}}$ (10\\%) & $\\delta^*_{\\text{sf}}$ & Relative bias at $\\delta{=}1$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\beta_1$ (primary) & 0.234 & $> 1.5$ & 39.7\\% \\\\\n$\\beta_2$ (secondary) & 0.154 & $> 1.5$ & 60.4\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{center}\n\nBoth coefficients are magnitude-unsafe ($\\delta^* < 1$), with the secondary coefficient more fragile ($\\delta^*{=}0.154$). If the same annotator were instead classifying a \\textbf{confounder} (e.g., media framing as a control variable), magnitude ASV rises to $\\delta^*{=}0.690$---a $3{\\times}$ increase in error tolerance from the same confusion matrix. This demonstrates the central insight: topology, not annotator quality, determines vulnerability. Algorithm~\\ref{alg:correction} routes this exposure topology to PPI++ or DSL correction; matrix-based methods (naive plug-in, MLA) are ineffective due to FWL non-applicability.\n\n\\subsection{End-to-End Demonstration}\n\\label{sec:e2e_demo}\n\n\\input{e2e_demo_table.tex}\n\nThe demonstration uses a symmetric $C_0$ ($d_{\\mathrm{rel}}{=}0$); empirical $K{=}3$ matrices deviate substantially (median $d_{\\mathrm{rel}}{=}0.33$, Remark~\\ref{rem:symmetry}), but core ASV ranking and correction routing remain valid under Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}.\n\n\\subsection{Semi-Real Case Study with Production LLM Confusion Matrices}\n\\label{sec:real_cm_demo}\n\nWe repeat the end-to-end pipeline using 10 confusion matrices estimated from production LLM annotation of VAST stance detection ($K{=}3$; DeepSeek, Llama4, Qwen2.5-7B, Qwen3 under zero-shot and few-shot prompting). These matrices exhibit median $d_{\\mathrm{rel}}{=}0.33$ (Remark~\\ref{rem:symmetry}), far from the symmetric assumption of Theorem~\\ref{thm:k3_symmetric}. Results confirm both topology-dependent patterns: PPI++ achieves 93.6\\% median exposure bias reduction while plug-in corrections yield 0\\% improvement for confounding (FWL invariance, machine precision; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e-real-cm}).\n\n\\input{e2e_real_cm_table.tex}\n\n\\subsection{Correction Method Sensitivity to $K$}\n\\label{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}\n\nWe test whether the correction method ranking from Table~\\ref{tab:correction} ($K{=}3$) is stable at $K{=}5$ and $K{=}10$. For each $K$, we sample 5 random Dirichlet confusion matrices (diagonal $\\geq 0.6$), run 100 MC replications per topology--matrix combination ($N{=}5{,}000$, 10\\% gold fraction), and compute RMSE reduction for all five methods across all seven topologies.\n\n\\paragraph{Grand ranking stability.}\nSpearman rank correlations across all topology--method pairs: $\\rho_{3,5} = 0.80$, $\\rho_{3,10} = 0.84$, $\\rho_{5,10} = 0.93$ (all $p < 10^{-8}$). Per-topology correlations are even stronger: 5 of 7 topologies (confounding, collider, M-bias, IV, and exposure at $K{=}10$) achieve $\\rho = 1.0$ between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}10$.\n\n\\paragraph{Method-specific patterns.}\nPPI++ effectiveness degrades with $K$: confounding RMSE reduction drops from 85.6\\% ($K{=}3$) to 75.6\\% ($K{=}10$); mediation drops from 57.1\\% to 9.0\\%. At $K{=}10$, PPI++ becomes harmful for IV ($-124\\%$) and exposure ($-11\\%$). MC-SIMEX is the most $K$-robust method, maintaining the top rank for 5/7 topologies at $K{=}10$ (vs.\\ 3/7 at $K{=}3$). FWL invariance (naive plug-in and MLA achieving 0\\% bias reduction on the treatment coefficient) holds at all $K$ tested, confirming the structural nature of this result.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Top correction method and RMSE reduction (\\%) by topology across $K$. Bold = top method for that topology--$K$ combination.}\n\\label{tab:correction_k}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & $K{=}3$ & $K{=}5$ & $K{=}10$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & \\textbf{PPI++} 85.6 & \\textbf{PPI++} 76.4 & \\textbf{PPI++} 75.6 \\\\\nMediation & \\textbf{PPI++} 57.1 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 59.7 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 59.0 \\\\\nCollider & \\textbf{SIMEX} 55.4 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 63.4 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 57.3 \\\\\nExposure & \\textbf{SIMEX} 72.5 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 65.0 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 47.3 \\\\\nM-bias & \\textbf{SIMEX} 3.2 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 47.0 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 43.7 \\\\\nIV & \\textbf{SIMEX} 30.9 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 30.4 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 27.1 \\\\\nFront-door & \\textbf{PPI++} 82.9 & \\textbf{PPI++} 69.0 & \\textbf{SIMEX} 70.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Comparison with Related Sensitivity Frameworks}\n\\label{sec:sensitivity_comparison}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Comparison of sensitivity frameworks.}\n\\label{tab:sensitivity_comparison}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\textbf{ASV} & \\textbf{E-value} & \\textbf{Partial} $R^2$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nParameter space & $K{\\times}K$ confusion $C(\\delta)$ & Scalar confounder & $R^2$ of omitted var \\\\\nSensitivity path & $(1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$ & Monotone scalar & Monotone scalar \\\\\nTopology-specific & \\checkmark & \\ding{55} & \\ding{55} \\\\\nSign-flip diagnostic & \\checkmark ($\\delta^*_{\\text{sign}}$) & \\ding{55} & \\ding{55} \\\\\nTopology dependence & \\checkmark (7 topologies ranked) & \\ding{55} & \\ding{55} \\\\\nMonotonicity & Proved $K{=}2$; conj.\\ $K{\\geq}3$ & Guaranteed & Guaranteed \\\\\nTarget & Annotation error & Unmeasured confounding & Omitted variable \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Per-Topology $F_1$--Bias Correlation}\n\\label{sec:within_topo_f1}\n\nThe cross-topology correlation between macro $F_1$ and $|\\text{bias}|$ is weak ($\\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$; \\S\\ref{sec:empirical}), but decomposing by topology reveals a structured pattern. Table~\\ref{tab:within_topo_f1} reports Spearman correlations computed over 23 LLM configurations (HumAID $K{=}10$, VAST $K{=}3$, CivilComments $K{=}2$; 161 total scenarios).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Within-topology Spearman $\\rho(F_1, |\\text{bias}|)$ across 23 empirical LLM configurations $\\times$ 3 datasets. Negative $\\rho$: higher $F_1$ $\\to$ lower bias (expected). Positive $\\rho$: higher $F_1$ $\\to$ higher bias (protective attenuation removed).}\n\\label{tab:within_topo_f1}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & $n$ & $\\rho$ & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & 23 & $-0.557$ & 0.006 \\\\\nMediation & 23 & $-0.565$ & 0.005 \\\\\nExposure & 23 & $-0.531$ & 0.009 \\\\\nFront-door & 23 & $-0.568$ & 0.005 \\\\\nIV & 23 & $-0.700$ & $<$0.001 \\\\\nCollider & 23 & $+0.310$ & 0.150 \\\\\nM-bias & 23 & $+0.867$ & $<$0.001 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nFive topologies show the expected negative correlation: better annotation quality ($F_1{\\uparrow}$) reduces causal bias. IV shows the strongest effect ($\\rho = -0.70$), consistent with the Wald estimator's sensitivity to misclassification in the first stage. M-bias uniquely reverses: $\\rho = +0.87$ ($p < 0.001$). The mechanism is that misclassification attenuates the spurious M-bias path, so the naive estimator under noisy annotations is \\emph{closer} to the true causal effect; improving annotation quality (higher $F_1$) removes this protective attenuation and \\emph{increases} bias. This is consistent with the correction results (\\S\\ref{sec:correction}), where PPI++ harms M-bias ($-$88\\%) by removing the same protective noise. Collider shows a positive but non-significant trend ($\\rho = +0.31$, $p = 0.15$), consistent with Berkson bias attenuation. Per-dataset breakdowns (CivilComments $K{=}2$: $\\rho = -1.0$ for all topologies with $n{=}7$) should be interpreted with caution given the small sample and limited $F_1$ variation within the binary task.\n\n\\subsection{Confusion Matrix Estimation Robustness}\n\\label{sec:c_robustness}\n\nThe ASV framework requires the confusion matrix $C$, which in practice must be estimated from a held-out gold-labeled subset. We assess how estimation noise in $\\hat{C}$ propagates to the safe/fragile partition and ASV ranking. For each of 8 empirical confusion matrices (VAST, 4 LLMs $\\times$ 2 prompting strategies), we draw $n_{\\text{gold}} \\in \\{50, 100, 200, 500\\}$ samples from a $\\text{Multinomial}(n_{\\text{gold}}, C_{\\cdot k})$ distribution per column, compute $\\hat{C}$, and evaluate the resulting ASV ranking across 200 bootstrap replicates.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{$\\hat{C}$ estimation robustness: safe/fragile partition stability and ASV ranking correlation (Spearman $\\rho$ vs.\\ true-$C$ ranking) under multinomial bootstrap from finite gold sets. 8 empirical matrices $\\times$ 200 bootstrap replicates per $n_{\\text{gold}}$.}\n\\label{tab:c_robustness}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n$n_{\\text{gold}}$ & Partition stability & $\\rho$ (median) & $\\rho$ ($q_{25}$) \\\\\n\\midrule\n50 & 50.4\\% & 0.991 & 0.789 \\\\\n100 & 82.0\\% & 1.000 & 0.991 \\\\\n200 & 85.6\\% & 1.000 & 0.991 \\\\\n500 & 88.5\\% & 1.000 & 0.991 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe ASV ranking is highly robust: median Spearman $\\rho \\geq 0.99$ even at $n_{\\text{gold}}{=}50$. The safe/fragile partition (5 safe vs.\\ 2 fragile topologies) recovers reliably from $n_{\\text{gold}} \\geq 100$ (82\\% stability), increasing to 89\\% at $n_{\\text{gold}}{=}500$. The remaining instability concentrates in source matrices with low diagonal dominance (e.g., VAST DeepSeek few-shot: $\\min C_{kk} = 0.16$), where bootstrap noise can shift borderline ASV values across the partition boundary. All 8 truth partitions match the canonical safe/fragile partition, confirming that the theoretical dichotomy holds empirically.\n\n\\subsection{Sign Reversal Determinants}\n\\label{sec:sign_determinants}\n\nWe investigate which confusion matrix features predict exposure sign reversal across the 23 empirical LLM configurations. For each matrix, we compute 10 structural features: $\\min_k C_{kk}$ (minimum diagonal), $\\overline{C_{kk}}$ (mean diagonal), $\\max_{j \\neq k} C_{jk}$ (maximum off-diagonal), $\\max_{j \\neq k} C_{jk}/C_{kk}$ (off-diagonal ratio), $\\log(\\kappa(C))$ (log condition number), mean and max column entropy, spectral gap, $\\det(C)$, and minimum singular value. Sign reversal is defined as any exposure coefficient reversing sign under misclassification.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Sign reversal rates by $K$ and best single-feature decision rule. Rule: $\\det(C) < 0.014 \\to$ sign reversal (accuracy 91.3\\%, sensitivity 85.7\\%, specificity 100\\%). An AND rule ($\\min_k C_{kk} < 0.55$ and mean entropy $> 0.40$) achieves 95.7\\% accuracy with 100\\% sensitivity.}\n\\label{tab:sign_determinants}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n$K$ & $n$ & Sign reversal rate \\\\\n\\midrule\n2 & 7 & 0/7 (0\\%) \\\\\n3 & 8 & 7/8 (87.5\\%) \\\\\n10 & 8 & 7/8 (87.5\\%) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nBinary matrices ($K{=}2$) never produce exposure sign reversal, consistent with classical attenuation theory. For $K \\geq 3$, the determinant of $C$ is the strongest single predictor: $\\det(C) < 0.014$ identifies sign-reversing matrices with 100\\% specificity (zero false positives) and 85.7\\% sensitivity. An AND rule combining $\\min_k C_{kk} < 0.55$ with mean column entropy $> 0.40$ achieves 100\\% sensitivity at 88.9\\% specificity. These rules are robust to $\\beta$-scaling (accuracy unchanged at $0.3{\\times}$ and $3{\\times}$ effect magnitude) but sensitive to $\\beta$-sign structure: under uniform-positive $\\beta$ (all coefficients same sign), no matrices produce sign reversal regardless of $C$, as cancellation across differently-signed coefficients is the underlying mechanism.\n\n\\subsection{Y-Stratified Non-Differential Verification}\n\\label{sec:y_stratified_test}\n\nWe directly test the non-differential assumption (Definition~\\ref{def:nondiff}) by stratifying confusion matrices by outcome-proxy variables and testing $C(Y{=}\\text{high}) \\approx C(Y{=}\\text{low})$. Using 12 LLM configurations on VAST ($K{=}3$; 4 models $\\times$ 3 prompting strategies, 1{,}500 samples each), we compute per-stratum confusion matrices under three Y-proxies and apply permutation tests ($B{=}10{,}000$, test statistic: $\\max_{j,k} |C^{(0)}_{jk} - C^{(1)}_{jk}|$).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Y-stratified non-differential test. Content-independent Y (text length) shows no significant violation (1/12 rejection $\\approx$ expected false positive rate at $\\alpha{=}0.05$). Content-dependent proxies (topic, sentiment) show expected violations since LLMs observe text content.}\n\\label{tab:y_stratified}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Y proxy} & \\textbf{Type} & \\textbf{Reject ($\\alpha{=}0.05$)} & \\textbf{Median $p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nText length & Content-indep. & 1/12 & 0.490 \\\\\nTopic & Content-dep. & 11/12 & 0.002 \\\\\nSentiment & Content-dep. & 10/12 & 0.017 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe single text-length rejection (Llama-4 few-shot-3, $p{=}0.027$) is consistent with the expected false positive rate ($0.05 \\times 12 = 0.6$). Content-dependent proxies (topic, sentiment) show systematic rejection because LLMs process text content from which these variables can be inferred, violating $Y \\perp \\text{text} \\mid A$. In causal inference applications, $Y$ is typically an external outcome (e.g., health status, economic indicator) that cannot be inferred from the annotated text, satisfying the independence condition. The content-dependent rejections confirm statistical power: the test can detect violations when they exist.\n\n\\section{Notation}\n\\label{app:notation_table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Key notation used throughout the paper.}\n\\label{tab:notation}\n\\begin{tabular}{cl}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Symbol} & \\textbf{Description} \\\\\n\\midrule\n$K$ & Number of annotation categories \\\\\n$C$ & Column-stochastic confusion matrix, $C_{jk} = P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k)$ \\\\\n$A$, $A^*$ & True and observed (misclassified) annotation \\\\\n$T$ & Treatment variable \\\\\n$Y$ & Outcome variable \\\\\n$\\tau$ & Causal effect of interest \\\\","numLines":270,"startLine":1249,"totalLines":1538}}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01My2udsS3GnFs9NYxbu4871","type":"tool_result","content":"The file /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/experiments.tex has been updated successfully."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"f8874594-f47e-4acf-8bb7-880f8df82032","uuid":"bc99e407-eea5-4196-a0cd-beca0710303f","timestamp":"2026-05-24T07:31:41.502Z","tool_use_result":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/experiments.tex","oldString":"\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nExposure & 85\\% & 83.4\\% & 14.1\\% & No \\\\\nMediation & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nFront-door & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nCollider & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nM-bias & 53.2\\% & 0\\% & 10.4\\% & Yes \\\\\nIV & 100\\% & 52.6\\% & 100\\% & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}","newString":"\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nExposure & 85\\% & 83.4\\% & 14.1\\% & No \\\\\nMediation & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nFront-door & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nCollider & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nM-bias & 53.2\\% & 0\\% & 10.4\\% & Yes \\\\\nIV & 100\\% & 52.6\\% & 100\\% & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}","originalFile":"\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe validate through Monte Carlo simulation ($K{=}3$), cross-$K$ robustness checks ($K{=}5,7$), and empirical validation with real LLM confusion matrices from three NLP tasks ($K{=}2,3,10$).\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nFor each topology, we sample 100{,}000 confusion matrices from a Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, compute analytical probability limits, and validate against Monte Carlo simulation ($N{=}10{,}000$). ASV experiments use $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$.\n\n\\subsection{Bias Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Theory-MC agreement.}\nAnalytical probability limits match Monte Carlo estimates with MAPE of 1.6\\%, averaged across 161 scenarios (23 LLM configurations $\\times$ 7 topologies; per-topology range: 0.3\\%--3.7\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure DAG: sign reversal under multi-class misclassification.}\nUnder realistic accuracy (diagonal $\\geq 0.7$), the violation rate is 13.7\\% with 2.1\\% sign reversal; at diagonal $\\geq 0.9$, violation persists at 11.9\\% (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}). Under the full Dirichlet prior, 85\\% of matrices violate the binary ``toward-null'' guarantee and 83.4\\% cause sign reversal; $\\min_k C_{kk} < 0.55$ predicts sign reversal with 91\\% accuracy (Appendix~\\ref{sec:sign_determinants}). The $K{=}2$ control confirms classical attenuation~\\cite{brenner1993}, establishing the multi-class failure as structurally distinct.\n\n\\paragraph{Structural guarantee for non-exposure topologies.}\nFive topologies exhibit zero sign-flip rate (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}): confounding, mediation, and front-door show 100\\% amplification~\\cite{brenner1993, dosemeci1990} but never reverse the sign; collider is protective ($|B(C)|$ non-increasing; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}); M-bias shows weakest sensitivity (53.2\\% violation). All patterns persist at $K{=}5,7$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cross_k_robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}\nThe IV topology is the sole non-exposure exception: 52.6\\% of random matrices cause sign reversal, stable across $K{=}3,5,7$. Diagonal dominance ($C_{kk} > 0.5$) eliminates sign reversal but preserves amplification (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}); however, all 23 empirical LLM configurations show zero IV sign reversal.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Bias under $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification (100{,}000 Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ matrices). Violation: $|\\text{bias}| > 0$; Sign Flip: any coefficient reverses sign; Amplif.: $|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$. $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$?: sign-flip guarantee holds unconditionally (Yes), conditionally (Cond.; Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip}), or not (No). Collider 100\\% amplification reflects Berkson bias---misclassification is protective.}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nExposure & 85\\% & 83.4\\% & 14.1\\% & No \\\\\nMediation & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nFront-door & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nCollider & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nM-bias & 53.2\\% & 0\\% & 10.4\\% & Yes \\\\\nIV & 100\\% & 52.6\\% & 100\\% & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe bias distributions are visualized in Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_distribution}.\n\n\\subsection{ASV Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:asv_results}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_asv_ranking.pdf}\n\\caption{ASV vulnerability ranking across topologies. Three ASV types (magnitude, significance, sign-flip) reveal a topology-dependent spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \\infty$).}\n\\label{fig:asv_ranking}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{ASV results.}\nThe ASV envelope is $\\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic for non-IV topologies ($\\varepsilon < 0.02$ for non-exposure under moderate effects, larger for exposure; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}). Figure~\\ref{fig:asv_ranking} shows the vulnerability ranking: IV is maximally fragile, M-bias is immune ($\\text{ASV} = \\infty$). Sign-flip ASV: 83.4\\% of exposure matrices yield $\\delta^*_{\\text{sf}} < 1$; IV ranges 1.1--6.7 (Table~\\ref{tab:asv_grid}). Significance ASV exhibits a phase transition at $N^* \\approx 6{,}000$--$10{,}000$; bootstrap CIs ($B{=}1{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv}) confirm stable topology ranking despite wide IV intervals.\n\n\\subsection{Empirical Validation}\n\\label{sec:empirical}\n\nWe validate using real LLM confusion matrices from HumAID~\\cite{alam2021humaid} ($K{=}10$), VAST ($K{=}3$), and CivilComments ($K{=}2$): four LLMs, 23 configurations, 161 bias scenarios.\n\n\\paragraph{Empirical bias ranking.}\nHumAID ($K{=}10$) matches predictions: IV (12.4--42.1\\%) and exposure (9.0--17.4\\%) dominate; remaining topologies $<$15\\%. $F_1$ shows no rank correlation with bias ($\\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$; Figure~\\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}), but per-topology patterns diverge: five topologies show $F_1{\\uparrow}{\\to}\\text{bias}{\\downarrow}$ ($\\rho \\leq -0.53$), while M-bias reverses ($\\rho = 0.87$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:within_topo_f1}). Of exposure coefficients, 14/23 (61\\%) exhibit sign reversal---all secondary; zero across 138 non-exposure scenarios.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}\n\\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not $F_1$. HumAID $K{=}10$ (56 points): within $F_1$ range 0.614--0.690, bias varies $10{\\times}$ (IV 42.1\\% vs.\\ collider $<$5.6\\%).}\n\\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Correction Guidance}\n\\label{sec:correction}\n\nWe evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, MLA, DSL~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, MC-SIMEX~\\cite{kuchenhoff2006simex}, PPI++~\\cite{angelopoulos2023}) across all topologies (210 configurations; Table~\\ref{tab:correction}, Appendix~\\ref{app:supplementary}). Regression calibration~\\cite{carroll2006} is designed for continuous measurement error and does not apply to categorical misclassification.\n\n\\paragraph{FWL invariance (observation).}\nFWL invariance implies matrix-based methods (naive plug-in, MLA) achieve 0\\% bias reduction for covariate topologies (Appendix~\\ref{app:fwl_proof}); under logistic regression this breaks but ranking persists ($|\\Delta| < 0.02$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}). To our knowledge, this futility result---that matrix-based corrections cannot reduce covariate-induced bias under FWL decomposition---has not been explicitly stated in the measurement error literature~\\cite{carroll2006, buonaccorsi2010}.\n\n\\paragraph{Method ranking.}\nPPI++ achieves the highest RMSE reduction (47.0\\% at $K{=}3$) but requires gold labels and harms M-bias ($-$88\\%); MC-SIMEX is gold-free and $K$-robust (27--70\\%), though it harms front-door ($-$62.4\\%); rankings are stable across $K$ ($\\rho \\geq 0.80$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}).\n\n\\paragraph{Correction decision procedure.}\nGiven topology $\\mathcal{T}$ and gold-label availability: (1)~\\textbf{Risk:} M-bias $\\to$ low; confounding/mediation/collider/front-door $\\to$ medium; exposure/IV $\\to$ high. (2)~\\textbf{Method:} with gold, PPI++ except M-bias; without gold, DSL for confounding/mediation, MC-SIMEX for IV/collider. (3)~\\textbf{FWL:} skip plug-in/MLA for covariate topologies (0\\% reduction). If $\\mathcal{T}$ uncertain, compute ASV under candidate topologies and report worst-case sensitivity (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:correction}, Appendix~\\ref{app:supplementary}).\n\n\\paragraph{Case studies and sensitivity.}\nClinicalBERT and HumAID ($K{=}10$) confirm all patterns---FWL invariance, PPI++ harm for M-bias/IV, partition recoverability---under linear and logistic models ($|\\Delta| < 0.02$; Appendix~\\ref{app:case_study},~\\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}). An end-to-end demo ($K{=}3$, $N{=}10{,}000$, 100 replicates; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e-demo}) confirms: confounding achieves 0\\% improvement (FWL), PPI++ reduces exposure bias from 42.9\\% to 0.2\\%, while topology-agnostic IV correction amplifies bias ($-$139\\%). A semi-real study with 10 VAST matrices ($d_{\\mathrm{rel}} = 0.30$--$0.38$) confirms: 93.6\\% median exposure bias reduction, 0\\% confounding improvement (Appendix~\\ref{sec:real_cm_demo}).\n","structuredPatch":[{"oldStart":28,"oldLines":7,"newStart":28,"newLines":7,"lines":[" \\caption{Bias under $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification (100{,}000 Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ matrices). Violation: $|\\text{bias}| > 0$; Sign Flip: any coefficient reverses sign; Amplif.: $|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$. $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$?: sign-flip guarantee holds unconditionally (Yes), conditionally (Cond.; Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip}), or not (No). Collider 100\\% amplification reflects Berkson bias---misclassification is protective.}"," \\label{tab:taxonomy}"," \\small","-\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}","+\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}"," \\toprule"," \\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\\\"," \\midrule"]},{"oldStart":40,"oldLines":7,"newStart":40,"newLines":7,"lines":[" M-bias & 53.2\\% & 0\\% & 10.4\\% & Yes \\\\"," IV & 100\\% & 52.6\\% & 100\\% & No \\\\"," \\bottomrule","-\\end{tabular}","+\\end{tabular}}"," \\end{table}"," "," The bias distributions are visualized in Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_distribution}."]}],"userModified":false,"replaceAll":false}}

\\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}","originalFile":"\\section{Proofs of Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip} and Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotone}}\n\\label{app:proof}\n\nWe provide algebraic proofs of sign preservation for the confounding and collider topologies (Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip}(a)--(b)), an explicit counterexample for exposure (Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip}(c)), numerical verification for mediation, M-bias, and front-door (Numerical Verification~1), and the monotonicity proof for confounding (Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotone}).\nThroughout, $A\\in\\{0,1,\\dots,K{-}1\\}$ with prior $p_k=P(A{=}k)$, and $C$ is a $K{\\times}K$ column-stochastic confusion matrix with $C_{jk}=P(A^*{=}j\\mid A{=}k)$.\nWe use category~0 as the reference and let $D_j=\\mathbf{1}(A{=}j)$, $D_j^*=\\mathbf{1}(A^*{=}j)$ for $j=1,\\dots,K{-}1$.\n\n\\subsection{Notation and Key Identities}\n\\label{app:notation}\n\nUnder non-differential misclassification, the misclassified moments factor linearly through $C$.\nDefine the population vectors:\n\\begin{align}\n  q_j &= P(A^*{=}j) = \\textstyle\\sum_k C_{jk}\\,p_k, \\label{eq:q}\\\\\n  \\mathrm{ct}_j &= \\mathbb{E}[T\\cdot D_j^*] = \\textstyle\\sum_k C_{jk}\\,\\mathbb{E}[T\\cdot D_k], \\label{eq:ct}\\\\\n  \\mathrm{cy}_j &= \\mathbb{E}[Y\\cdot D_j^*] = \\textstyle\\sum_k C_{jk}\\,\\mathbb{E}[Y\\cdot D_k]. \\label{eq:cy}\n\\end{align}\nWrite $q = Cp$, $\\mathrm{ct} = C\\,\\mathbb{E}[T\\cdot D]$, and $\\mathrm{cy} = C\\,\\mathbb{E}[Y\\cdot D]$, where $D=(D_0,\\dots,D_{K-1})^\\top$.\n\n\\subsection{Frisch--Waugh--Lovell Decomposition}\n\\label{app:fwl}\n\nFor topologies where $T$ is directly observed (confounding, mediation, collider, M-bias, front-door), the estimator regresses $Y$ on $Z^*=(1,T,D_1^*,\\dots,D_{K-1}^*)$.\nBy the Frisch--Waugh--Lovell (FWL) theorem, the coefficient on $T$ satisfies:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\operatorname{plim}\\hat{\\tau}(C)\n  = \\frac{\\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}\\,\\tilde{Y}]}{\\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}^2]},\n  \\label{eq:fwl}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\tilde{T}=T-\\mathbb{E}[T\\mid A^*]$ and $\\tilde{Y}=Y-\\mathbb{E}[Y\\mid A^*]$ are residuals from projecting onto $(1,D_1^*,\\dots,D_{K-1}^*)$.\n\n\\begin{lemma}[Bias decomposition]\n\\label{lem:bias_decomp}\nSuppose $Y = \\tau T + f(A) + \\varepsilon_Y$ with $\\varepsilon_Y \\perp (T, A, A^*)$.\nThen\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau(C) = \\tau + \\frac{R(C)}{D(C)},\n  \\label{eq:bias_decomp}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere\n\\begin{align}\n  R(C) &= \\mathbb{E}\\bigl[\\operatorname{Cov}(T,\\,f(A)\\mid A^*)\\bigr], \\label{eq:R}\\\\\n  D(C) &= \\mathbb{E}\\bigl[\\operatorname{Var}(T\\mid A^*)\\bigr] > 0. \\label{eq:D}\n\\end{align}\n\\end{lemma}\n\n\\begin{proof}\nExpand $\\tilde{Y}$ using $Y=\\tau T + f(A) + \\varepsilon_Y$:\n\\[\n  \\tilde{Y} = \\tau\\,\\tilde{T} + \\bigl(f(A) - \\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid A^*]\\bigr) + \\varepsilon_Y.\n\\]\nSince $\\tilde{T}$ is measurable w.r.t.\\ $(T,A^*)$ and $\\varepsilon_Y\\perp(T,A,A^*)$:\n\\[\n  \\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}\\,\\tilde{Y}]\n  = \\tau\\,\\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}^2]\n  + \\mathbb{E}\\bigl[\\tilde{T}\\,\\bigl(f(A)-\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid A^*]\\bigr)\\bigr].\n\\]\nThe numerator residual is $R(C) = \\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}(f(A)-\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid A^*])]$, and the denominator is $D(C) = \\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}^2]$.\n\nBy the law of total covariance:\n\\[\n  R(C) = \\sum_{j=0}^{K-1} q_j\\,\\operatorname{Cov}(T,\\,f(A)\\mid A^*{=}j)\n  = \\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A)\\mid A^*)].\n\\]\nFor $D(C)>0$: decompose $T = g(A) + \\varepsilon_T$ where $g(A)=\\mathbb{E}[T\\mid A]$ (for confounding) or simply note that $T$ has exogenous variation independent of $A^*$ (for mediation).\nIn both cases, $\\tilde{T}=\\bigl(g(A)-\\mathbb{E}[g(A)\\mid A^*]\\bigr)+\\varepsilon_T'$, where $\\varepsilon_T'$ is the component of $T$ independent of $(A,A^*)$.\nHence $D(C)\\geq\\operatorname{Var}(\\varepsilon_T')>0$.\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\subsection{Confounding Topology ($A$ = Confounder)}\n\\label{app:confounding}\n\n\\paragraph{Data-generating process.}\n$A\\to T$, $A\\to Y$, $T\\to Y$.\nUsing dummy coding with category 0 as reference:\n\\begin{align*}\n  T &= \\sum_{j=1}^{K-1}\\alpha_j D_j + \\varepsilon_T,\\quad\n  \\varepsilon_T\\sim\\mathcal{N}(0,\\sigma_T^2),\\;\\varepsilon_T\\perp A,\\\\\n  Y &= \\tau\\, T + \\sum_{j=1}^{K-1}\\beta_j^{(A)} D_j + \\varepsilon_Y,\\quad\n  \\varepsilon_Y\\sim\\mathcal{N}(0,1),\\;\\varepsilon_Y\\perp(T,A).\n\\end{align*}\nDefine $g(k)=\\sum_{j}\\alpha_j\\mathbf{1}(k{=}j)$ and $f(k)=\\sum_{j}\\beta_j^{(A)}\\mathbf{1}(k{=}j)$, so $g(0)=f(0)=0$.\n\n\\paragraph{Closed-form plim.}\nUsing the FWL decomposition, the coefficient on $T$ is:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau(C)\n  = \\frac{\\mu_{TY} - \\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\\frac{\\mathrm{ct}_j\\,\\mathrm{cy}_j}{q_j}}\n  {s_T - \\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\\frac{\\mathrm{ct}_j^2}{q_j}},\n  \\label{eq:plim_conf}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $s_T=\\mathbb{E}[T^2]$, $\\mu_{TY}=\\mathbb{E}[TY]$, and $\\mathrm{ct}_j$, $\\mathrm{cy}_j$, $q_j$ are defined in~\\eqref{eq:q}--\\eqref{eq:cy}.\n\n\\begin{proof}[Derivation]\nBy FWL, $\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau = \\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}Y]/\\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}^2]$ where $\\tilde{T}=T-\\mathbb{E}[T\\mid A^*]$.\nSince $\\mathbb{E}[T\\mid A^*{=}j]=\\mathrm{ct}_j/q_j$:\n\\begin{align*}\n  \\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}^2]\n  &= \\mathbb{E}[T^2] - \\sum_j q_j\\bigl(\\mathrm{ct}_j/q_j\\bigr)^2\n  = s_T - \\sum_j \\mathrm{ct}_j^2/q_j,\\\\\n  \\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}\\,Y]\n  &= \\mathbb{E}[TY] - \\sum_j \\bigl(\\mathrm{ct}_j/q_j\\bigr)\\,\\mathbb{E}[Y\\cdot D_j^*]\n  = \\mu_{TY} - \\sum_j \\mathrm{ct}_j\\,\\mathrm{cy}_j/q_j.\n\\end{align*}\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\paragraph{Verification at $C=I$.}\nWhen $C=I$: $q_j=p_j$, $\\mathrm{ct}_j=\\mathbb{E}[T D_j]=\\alpha_j p_j$, $\\mathrm{cy}_j=\\mathbb{E}[Y D_j]=p_j(\\tau\\alpha_j+\\beta_j^{(A)})$.\nDirect substitution yields numerator $= \\tau\\sigma_T^2$ and denominator $= \\sigma_T^2$, giving $\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau = \\tau$.\n\n\\paragraph{Bias structure.}\nBy Lemma~\\ref{lem:bias_decomp}:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau(C) = \\tau + B(C),\\quad\n  B(C) = \\frac{\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(g(A),\\,f(A)\\mid A^*)]}{D(C)}.\n  \\label{eq:bias_conf}\n\\end{equation}\nBy the law of total covariance:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(g,f\\mid A^*)]\n  = \\operatorname{Cov}(g(A),f(A))\n  - \\operatorname{Cov}\\bigl(\\mathbb{E}[g(A)\\mid A^*],\\;\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid A^*]\\bigr).\n  \\label{eq:lotc}\n\\end{equation}\n\nAt $C=I$: $\\mathbb{E}[g\\mid A^*]=g(A)$, so the between-group covariance equals $\\operatorname{Cov}(g,f)$ and $B(I)=0$.\n\nAt $C=\\frac{1}{K}\\mathbf{1}\\mathbf{1}^\\top$ (uniform, $A^*\\perp A$): $\\mathbb{E}[g\\mid A^*]$ is constant, so the between-group covariance is zero and\n\\begin{equation}\n  B_{\\mathrm{OV}} \\;\\equiv\\; B\\bigl(\\tfrac{1}{K}\\mathbf{1}\\mathbf{1}^\\top\\bigr)\n  = \\frac{\\operatorname{Cov}(g(A),\\,f(A))}{\\operatorname{Var}(T)},\n  \\label{eq:bov}\n\\end{equation}\nwhich equals the classical omitted-variable bias (no control for $A$).\n\n\\paragraph{Sign preservation condition.}\nSince $D(C)>0$, sign$(\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau) = $ sign$(\\tau\\cdot D(C) + R(C))$.\nFor sign preservation, it suffices that $R(C)$ does not overwhelm $\\tau\\cdot D(C)$.\n\n\\begin{proposition}[Confounding sign preservation --- proportional case]\n\\label{prop:conf_prop}\nIf $f(A) = \\lambda\\,g(A)$ for some constant $\\lambda$ (i.e., the confounder effects on $T$ and $Y$ are proportional), then\n\\begin{equation}\n  B(C) = \\bigl(1-\\xi(C)\\bigr)\\,B_{\\mathrm{OV}},\n  \\label{eq:convex}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere\n\\[\n  \\xi(C) = \\frac{\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g(A)\\mid A^*])\\;\\sigma_T^2}\n  {\\operatorname{Var}(g(A))\\bigl(\\operatorname{Var}(g(A))-\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g(A)\\mid A^*])+\\sigma_T^2\\bigr)}\n  \\;\\in\\;[0,1].\n\\]\nConsequently, $B(C)$ lies between $0$ and $B_{\\mathrm{OV}}$, and $\\operatorname{sign}(\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau)=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)$ whenever $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau+B_{\\mathrm{OV}})$.\n\\end{proposition}\n\n\\begin{proof}\nWhen $f=\\lambda g$: the between-group covariance in~\\eqref{eq:lotc} becomes $\\lambda\\,\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g\\mid A^*])$, and $\\operatorname{Cov}(g,f)=\\lambda\\,\\operatorname{Var}(g)$.\nSo $R(C)=\\lambda\\bigl(\\operatorname{Var}(g)-\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g\\mid A^*])\\bigr)=\\lambda\\,V_W$, where $V_W=\\operatorname{Var}(g)-V_B$ with $V_B=\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g\\mid A^*])$.\nSimilarly, $D(C)=V_W+\\sigma_T^2$.\nHence\n\\[\n  B(C) = \\frac{\\lambda\\,V_W}{V_W+\\sigma_T^2},\\qquad\n  B_{\\mathrm{OV}} = \\frac{\\lambda\\,V}{V+\\sigma_T^2},\n\\]\nwhere $V=\\operatorname{Var}(g)=V_B+V_W$.\nThe ratio\n\\[\n  \\frac{B(C)}{B_{\\mathrm{OV}}} = \\frac{V_W(V+\\sigma_T^2)}{V(V_W+\\sigma_T^2)} = 1 - \\xi(C)\n\\]\nis a decreasing function of $V_B\\in[0,V]$.\nAt $V_B=0$ (no classification, $C$ uniform): $\\xi=0$, $B=B_{\\mathrm{OV}}$.\nAt $V_B=V$ (perfect classification, $C=I$): $\\xi=1$, $B=0$.\nSince $0\\leq V_B\\leq V$, we have $\\xi\\in[0,1]$ and $B(C)\\in[0,B_{\\mathrm{OV}}]$ (with the sign of $B_{\\mathrm{OV}}$).\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\begin{proposition}[Confounding sign preservation --- general case]\n\\label{prop:conf_gen}\nFor general $g$ and $f$ (not necessarily proportional), define the worst-case bias bound\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\bar{B} \\;=\\; \\frac{\\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g(A))\\,\\operatorname{Var}(f(A))}}{\\sigma_T^2}.\n  \\label{eq:bbar}\n\\end{equation}\nIf $|\\tau| > \\bar{B}$, then $\\operatorname{sign}(\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau(C))=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)$ for \\emph{all} column-stochastic $C$.\n\\end{proposition}\n\n\\begin{proof}\nWe need $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau\\cdot D(C)+R(C))=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)$, which holds if $|R(C)|<|\\tau|\\cdot D(C)$.\nSince $D(C)\\geq\\sigma_T^2$ (shown in Lemma~\\ref{lem:bias_decomp}), it suffices to show $|R(C)|<|\\tau|\\,\\sigma_T^2$.\n\n\\emph{Step 1: Bound $|R(C)|$.}\n$R(C)=\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(g(A),f(A)\\mid A^*)]$.\nBy the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality applied to each within-group covariance:\n\\[\n  |\\operatorname{Cov}(g,f\\mid A^*{=}j)|\n  \\leq \\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g\\mid A^*{=}j)\\,\\operatorname{Var}(f\\mid A^*{=}j)}.\n\\]\nTaking expectations and applying Cauchy--Schwarz again:\n\\begin{align*}\n  |R(C)|\n  &\\leq \\mathbb{E}\\bigl[\\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g\\mid A^*)\\,\\operatorname{Var}(f\\mid A^*)}\\bigr]\\\\\n  &\\leq \\sqrt{\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(g\\mid A^*)]\\;\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(f\\mid A^*)]}\\\\\n  &\\leq \\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g(A))\\;\\operatorname{Var}(f(A))},\n\\end{align*}\nwhere the last step uses $\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(X\\mid Y)]\\leq\\operatorname{Var}(X)$ (law of total variance).\n\n\\emph{Step 2: Conclude.}\nThe bias satisfies\n\\[\n  |B(C)| = \\frac{|R(C)|}{D(C)} \\leq \\frac{\\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g)\\,\\operatorname{Var}(f)}}{\\sigma_T^2} = \\bar{B}.\n\\]\nWhen $|\\tau|>\\bar{B}$, the bias cannot cancel $\\tau$, so $\\operatorname{sign}(\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau)=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)$ for all $C$.\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship to the omitted-variable condition.}\nA weaker (necessary) condition for sign preservation is $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{OV}})$ where $\\tau_{\\mathrm{OV}}=\\tau+\\operatorname{Cov}(g,f)/\\operatorname{Var}(T)$.\nIf this fails, then $C=\\frac{1}{K}\\mathbf{1}\\mathbf{1}^\\top$ (uniform noise) already flips the sign.\nThe rigorous sufficient condition $|\\tau|>\\bar{B}$ implies the weaker condition, since $|B_{\\mathrm{OV}}|=|\\operatorname{Cov}(g,f)|/\\operatorname{Var}(T)\\leq\\bar{B}$ by Cauchy--Schwarz and $\\operatorname{Var}(T)\\geq\\sigma_T^2$.\n\n\\paragraph{Verification for paper parameters ($K{=}3$).}\nWith $\\tau{=}2.0$, $\\boldsymbol{\\alpha}{=}(1.5,{-}1.0)$, $\\boldsymbol{\\beta}^{(A)}{=}(1.0,{-}0.5)$, $\\sigma_T{=}1.0$, $\\mathbf{p}{=}(0.40,0.35,0.25)$:\n\\begin{align*}\n  \\operatorname{Var}(g) &= \\sum_k p_k\\alpha_k^2 - \\bigl(\\textstyle\\sum_k p_k\\alpha_k\\bigr)^2 = 0.962,\\\\\n  \\operatorname{Var}(f) &= \\sum_k p_k(\\beta_k^{(A)})^2 - \\bigl(\\textstyle\\sum_k p_k\\beta_k^{(A)}\\bigr)^2 = 0.362,\\\\\n  \\bar{B} &= \\sqrt{0.962\\times 0.362}\\;/\\;1.0^2 = 0.590.\n\\end{align*}\nSince $|\\tau|{=}2.0 > \\bar{B}{=}0.590$, sign preservation is guaranteed for \\emph{all} column-stochastic $C$ by Proposition~\\ref{prop:conf_gen}.\nAdditionally, $B_{\\mathrm{OV}}=\\operatorname{Cov}(g,f)/\\operatorname{Var}(T)=0.588/1.962={+}0.300$, giving $\\tau_{\\mathrm{OV}}{=}2.300>0$, confirming that both the necessary and sufficient conditions are satisfied.\n\n\\paragraph{Extension to $K > 3$.}\nFor $K{>}3$, treatment effect coefficients are drawn from a fixed pool with alternating signs and decreasing magnitudes: $\\boldsymbol{\\beta} = (0, 1.0, {-}0.5, 0.7, {-}0.3, 0.5, {-}0.2, 0.4, {-}0.15, 0.3, \\ldots)$, truncated to $K$ entries. The category distribution uses $p_k \\propto 1/(k{+}1)$, normalized to sum to 1. Other topology-specific parameters (confounding strengths, mediator effects, etc.) follow analogous alternating-sign pools. For $K{=}10$, $\\boldsymbol{\\beta} = (0, 1.0, {-}0.5, 0.7, {-}0.3, 0.5, {-}0.2, 0.4, {-}0.15, 0.3)$.\n\n\\subsection{Proof of Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotone} (Confounding Monotonicity)}\n\\label{app:monotonicity_proof}\n\nUnder the interpolation $C(\\delta)=(1{-}\\delta)I+\\delta C_0$, we prove $|B(C(\\delta))|$ is non-decreasing in $\\delta$ for proportional effects ($f=\\lambda g$) and provide numerical verification for the general case.\nThe proof proceeds by direct algebraic analysis of $V_B(\\delta)=\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g(A)\\mid A^*_\\delta])$ as a rational function of $\\delta$, establishing convexity and bounding the derivative at both endpoints.\n\n\\paragraph{Proportional case ($f=\\lambda g$).}\nFrom Proposition~\\ref{prop:conf_prop}, $B(C)=\\lambda V_W/(V_W+\\sigma_T^2)$ where $V_W=\\operatorname{Var}(g)-V_B$ and $V_B(\\delta)=\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g(A)\\mid A^*_\\delta])$.\n\n\\emph{Step 1: Convexity of $V_B(\\delta)$.}\nDefine $N_j(\\delta)=\\sum_k g_k\\,C(\\delta)_{jk}\\,p_k$ and $q_j(\\delta)=\\sum_k C(\\delta)_{jk}\\,p_k$.\nBoth are affine in $\\delta$, with $q_j(\\delta)>0$.\nThen $V_B(\\delta)=\\sum_j N_j(\\delta)^2/q_j(\\delta) - \\bar{g}^2$.\nEach term $N_j^2/q_j$ is the composition of the perspective function $(x,t)\\mapsto x^2/t$ (convex for $t>0$) with the affine map $\\delta\\mapsto(N_j(\\delta),\\,q_j(\\delta))$, hence convex in $\\delta$.\nA sum of convex functions is convex, so $V_B(\\delta)$ is convex on $[0,\\delta_{\\max}]$.\n\n\\emph{Step 2: Derivative formula.}\nLet $\\mu_j(\\delta)=N_j(\\delta)/q_j(\\delta)=\\mathbb{E}[g(A)\\mid A^*{=}j]$.\nThen\n\\begin{equation}\n\\frac{dV_B}{d\\delta}\n= -\\sum_{j,k}\\bigl[(C_0)_{jk}-\\mathbf{1}_{j=k}\\bigr]\\,p_k\\,(g_k-\\mu_j(\\delta))^2.\n\\label{eq:dVB}\n\\end{equation}\n\\emph{Derivation.}\n$\\frac{d}{d\\delta}\\frac{N_j^2}{q_j}=2\\mu_j\\dot{N}_j-\\mu_j^2\\dot{q}_j$\nwhere $\\dot{N}_j=m_j^0-g_jp_j$ and $\\dot{q}_j=q_j^0-p_j$ (with $m_j^0=\\sum_k g_k(C_0)_{jk}p_k$, $q_j^0=\\sum_k(C_0)_{jk}p_k$).\nSubstituting and using column-stochasticity ($\\sum_j(C_0)_{jk}=\\sum_j\\mathbf{1}_{j=k}=1$) to cancel the $g_k^2$ terms yields~\\eqref{eq:dVB}.\n\n\\emph{Step 3: $V_B'(0)\\leq 0$ (universal, no conditions on $C_0$).}\nAt $\\delta{=}0$: $\\mu_j(0)=g_j$ (since $C(0)=I$), so $(g_k-\\mu_j(0))^2=(g_k-g_j)^2$.\nThe diagonal terms ($j{=}k$) vanish.\nHence\n\\[\nV_B'(0)=-\\sum_{j\\neq k}(C_0)_{jk}\\,p_k\\,(g_j-g_k)^2\\;\\leq\\; 0,\n\\]\nsince every term is non-negative.\n\n\\emph{Step 4: $V_B'(1)\\leq 0$ for $K{=}2$.}\nFor $K{=}2$ with $C_0=\\bigl[\\begin{smallmatrix}a&1{-}b\\\\1{-}a&b\\end{smallmatrix}\\bigr]$ and $a,b\\geq\\tfrac{1}{2}$,\nset $g_0{=}0$, $g_1{=}1$ without loss of generality\n(the general case scales by $(\\Delta g)^2$).\nAt $\\delta{=}1$: $q_0{=}ap_0{+}(1{-}b)p_1$, $q_1{=}(1{-}a)p_0{+}bp_1$,\n$\\mu_0{=}(1{-}b)p_1/q_0$, $\\mu_1{=}bp_1/q_1$.\nExpanding~\\eqref{eq:dVB} at $\\delta{=}1$ and factoring:\n\\begin{align*}\n  V_B'(1) &= (1{-}a)\\,p_0(\\mu_0^2{-}\\mu_1^2)+(1{-}b)\\,p_1\\bigl[(1{-}\\mu_1)^2{-}(1{-}\\mu_0)^2\\bigr] \\\\\n  &= (\\mu_0{-}\\mu_1)\\,\\bigl[(1{-}a)\\,p_0(\\mu_0{+}\\mu_1)+(1{-}b)\\,p_1(2{-}\\mu_0{-}\\mu_1)\\bigr].\n\\end{align*}\nThe bracket is non-negative (since $0\\leq\\mu_j\\leq 1$ and all coefficients are non-negative).\nDirect calculation gives $\\mu_0{-}\\mu_1=p_0\\,p_1(1{-}a{-}b)/(q_0\\,q_1)$\n(expand $(1{-}b)\\,q_1-b\\,q_0=p_0(1{-}a{-}b)$).\nUnder $a,b\\geq\\tfrac{1}{2}$: $a{+}b\\geq 1$, so $\\mu_0{-}\\mu_1\\leq 0$ and $V_B'(1)\\leq 0$.\n\n\\emph{Step 5: Monotonicity from convexity.}\nSince $V_B$ is convex (Step~1), its derivative $V_B'$ is non-decreasing.\nWhen $V_B'(1)\\leq 0$, we have $V_B'(\\delta)\\leq V_B'(1)\\leq 0$ for all $\\delta\\in[0,1]$, so $V_B$ is non-increasing.\n\n\\emph{Step 6: $|B(\\delta)|$ is non-decreasing.}\n$V_W(\\delta)=\\operatorname{Var}(g)-V_B(\\delta)$ is non-decreasing (Step~5).\nThe function $h(x)=|\\lambda|\\,x/(x+\\sigma_T^2)$ is strictly increasing for $x\\geq 0$.\nHence $|B(\\delta)|=h(V_W(\\delta))$ is non-decreasing. \\qed\n\n\\paragraph{Condition on $C_0$.}\nThe proof requires $V_B'(1)\\leq 0$, which is guaranteed for $K{=}2$ when $(C_0)_{jj}\\geq\\tfrac{1}{2}$ (Step~4).\nFor $K\\geq 3$, $V_B'(1)\\leq 0$ holds for all $g$ and $p$ if and only if a certain matrix $M-W$ (depending on $C_0$ and $p$) is negative semi-definite; simple diagonal thresholds are sufficient in practice but not universal.\nNumerical verification across 20{,}000 random $(C_0,p,g)$ triples per $K\\in\\{3,5,7\\}$ with $(C_0)_{jj}\\geq 1/K$ finds worst-case $|B|$ step deviations $< 0.013$ (Table~\\ref{tab:conjecture_verify}), confirming $\\varepsilon$-approximate monotonicity.\nA $K{=}2$ counterexample ($C_0$ the permutation matrix, $(C_0)_{jj}{=}0$) shows the diagonal condition is necessary: $V_B(\\delta)=(\\delta{-}\\tfrac{1}{2})^2$ increases on $(\\tfrac{1}{2},1]$.\n\n\\paragraph{General case (non-proportional effects).}\n$|B(C(\\delta))|\\leq\\bar{B}(\\delta)$ where $\\bar{B}(\\delta)=\\sqrt{\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(g\\mid A^*_\\delta)]\\,\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(f\\mid A^*_\\delta)]}\\,/\\,D(\\delta)$ (Proposition~\\ref{prop:conf_gen}).\nBy the same convexity argument, each $\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(\\cdot\\mid A^*_\\delta)]$ is non-decreasing in $\\delta$, and $D(\\delta)=\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(T\\mid A^*_\\delta)]$ is non-decreasing.\nExtended verification (20{,}000 random $C_0$ matrices, $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$) reveals worst-case step deviations of 0.011 (confounding), confirming approximate tightness; a strict monotonicity proof for the general case remains open.\n\n\\begin{remark}[Conditional exact monotonicity]\n\\label{rem:conditional_monotonicity}\nExact monotonicity ($V_B'(\\delta) \\leq 0$ for all $\\delta$) holds under the \\emph{component-wise diagonal dominance} condition:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\alpha_j \\geq \\frac{q_j}{p_j + q_j} \\quad \\text{for all } j,\n  \\label{eq:dd_condition}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\alpha_j = (C_0)_{jj}$ and $q_j = \\sum_k (C_0)_{jk} p_k$. This condition requires that each class's classification accuracy exceeds a threshold determined by its spillover ratio $q_j/p_j$. The proof uses Cauchy--Schwarz on the endpoint derivative decomposition $V_B'(1) = \\sum_j (p_j + q_j)\\,e_j^2 - \\sum_{j \\neq k} (C_0)_{jk}\\,p_k\\,(g_j - g_k)^2$, where $e_j = g_j - \\mu_j$ (full proof in supplementary material).\n\nFor doubly stochastic $C_0$ with uniform $p$, condition~\\eqref{eq:dd_condition} reduces to $\\alpha_j \\geq 1/2$, which for $K{=}2$ recovers the exact condition in Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotone}. Numerical testing ($10^5$ random column-stochastic matrices with $\\alpha_j \\geq 0.5$) shows the condition holds in $99.3\\%$ of cases; the $0.7\\%$ violations arise predominantly under class imbalance with maximum step deviation $< 0.001$. When the condition fails, a quantitative bound $V_B'(1) \\leq (1{-}c)^2 R_g^2$ (where $c = \\min_j \\alpha_j$ and $R_g = \\max g_k - \\min g_k$) ensures violations vanish as classifier accuracy improves.\n\\end{remark}\n\n\\subsection{Mediation Topology ($A$ = Mediator)}\n\\label{app:mediation}\n\n\\paragraph{Data-generating process.}\n$T\\to A\\to Y$, $T\\to Y$.\n\\begin{align*}\n  T &\\sim \\operatorname{Bernoulli}(0.5),\\\\\n  A\\mid T &\\sim \\operatorname{Categorical}\\bigl(\\operatorname{softmax}(\\boldsymbol{\\gamma}+\\boldsymbol{\\delta}\\,T)\\bigr),\\\\\n  Y &= \\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}\\,T + f(A) + \\varepsilon_Y.\n\\end{align*}\n\n\\paragraph{Bias decomposition.}\nBy Lemma~\\ref{lem:bias_decomp}, the direct-effect estimator satisfies:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}(C) = \\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}} + \\frac{\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(T,\\,f(A)\\mid A^*)]}{D(C)}.\n  \\label{eq:med_bias}\n\\end{equation}\n\nThe key structural difference from confounding: $T$ causes $A$, so the residual covariance $\\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A)\\mid A^*)$ captures the indirect effect of $T$ on $Y$ through $A$ that is not absorbed by conditioning on $A^*$.\n\n\\paragraph{Endpoint analysis.}\n\nAt $C=I$ ($A^*{=}A$): conditioning on $A$ blocks the mediated path $T\\to A\\to Y$, so $\\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A)\\mid A)=0$ and $\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau=\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}$.\n\nAt $C=\\frac{1}{K}\\mathbf{1}\\mathbf{1}^\\top$ ($A^*\\perp A$): no information about $A$ is used, and\n\\begin{align*}\n  \\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A)\\mid A^*)] &= \\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A)),\\\\\n  D(C) &= \\operatorname{Var}(T) = 0.25.\n\\end{align*}\nThe indirect effect is $\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}=\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid T{=}1]-\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid T{=}0]$, and\n\\[\n  \\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A)) = 0.25\\,\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}.\n\\]\nHence at $C$ uniform: $\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau = \\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}+\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}} = \\tau_{\\mathrm{total}}$.\n\n\\paragraph{Numerical Verification (Mediation sign preservation).}\n\\label{find:med_sign}\n$\\operatorname{sign}(\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}})=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}})$ whenever $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}})=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{total}})$, where $\\tau_{\\mathrm{total}}=\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}+\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}$.\nThis result is verified numerically for $100{,}000$ independently sampled column-stochastic matrices $C$; a closed-form proof for general $K$ remains open.\n\n\\begin{proof}\nLet $R(C)=\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A)\\mid A^*)]$ and $D(C)=\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(T\\mid A^*)]>0$.\n\n\\emph{Endpoints.}\nAt $C{=}I$: $f(A)$ is constant given $A{=}A^*$, so $R(I)=0$ and $B(I)=0$.\nAt $C=\\frac{1}{K}\\mathbf{1}\\mathbf{1}^\\top$: $R=\\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A))=0.25\\,\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}$ and $D=\\operatorname{Var}(T)=0.25$, giving $B=\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}$.\n\n\\emph{Within-group structure for binary $T$.}\nSince $T\\in\\{0,1\\}$, the within-$A^*$-group bias admits a revealing decomposition.\nLet $p_j=P(T{=}1\\mid A^*{=}j)$.\nThen $\\operatorname{Var}(T\\mid A^*{=}j)=p_j(1{-}p_j)$ and\n\\[\n  \\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A)\\mid A^*{=}j)\n  = p_j(1{-}p_j)\\bigl[\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid T{=}1,A^*{=}j]-\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid T{=}0,A^*{=}j]\\bigr].\n\\]\nDefine $\\delta_j=\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid T{=}1,A^*{=}j]-\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid T{=}0,A^*{=}j]$ as the \\emph{conditional indirect effect} in the $A^*{=}j$ subpopulation.\nThen\n\\[\n  B(C)=\\frac{R(C)}{D(C)}\n  =\\frac{\\sum_j q_j\\,p_j(1{-}p_j)\\,\\delta_j}{\\sum_j q_j\\,p_j(1{-}p_j)}\n  =\\sum_j w_j\\,\\delta_j,\n\\]\na weighted average of the conditional indirect effects, with weights $w_j=q_j p_j(1{-}p_j)/\\sum_\\ell q_\\ell p_\\ell(1{-}p_\\ell)\\geq 0$ summing to 1.\n\nAt $C{=}I$: each $A^*{=}j$ group has $A{=}j$ a.s., so $\\delta_j=f(j)-f(j)=0$ and $B=0$.\nAt $C$ uniform: $\\delta_j=\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}$ for all $j$ and $B=\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}$.\n\n\\emph{Sign preservation.}\nWhen $\\operatorname{Cov}(g(A),f(A))>0$ (the indirect effect reinforces the direct effect, i.e., $\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}$ and $\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}$ have the same sign), the bias $B(C)\\geq 0$ cannot oppose $\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}$, so sign is preserved trivially.\n\nWhen $\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}$ and $\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}$ have opposite signs but $|\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}|<|\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}|$ (i.e., $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}})=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{total}})$), sign reversal requires some $|\\delta_j|>|\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}|$ with the opposing sign.\nSince each $\\delta_j$ is a difference of weighted averages of $f$ values (with weights $P(A{=}k\\mid T{=}t,A^*{=}j)$), and $B(C)$ is a convex combination of $\\delta_j$, the bound $|B(C)|\\leq\\max_j|\\delta_j|$ holds.\nWe verify numerically that $\\max_j|\\delta_j|\\leq|\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}|$ for all 100{,}000 random $C$ matrices in our experiments, confirming sign preservation.\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\paragraph{Verification for paper parameters ($K{=}3$).}\nWith $\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}{=}0.5$, $\\boldsymbol{\\gamma}{=}(0,0,0)$, $\\boldsymbol{\\delta}{=}(0,0.8,{-}0.6)$, $\\boldsymbol{\\beta}{=}(0,1.0,{-}0.5)$:\n\n$\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}=\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid T{=}1]-\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid T{=}0]=0.351$ (computed from the softmax
